Many fact-checking approaches frame misinformation resistance as a binary judgment: something is either true or false, real or fake (Maertens et al., 2024), or manipulative or not (Said et al., 2025). While this framing has practical and methodological appeal, it may not be the most tractable target for inductive learning. The universe of false and misleading claims is vast and ever-changing, making it difficult to build general misinformation resistance merely from a set of falsehoods. Furthermore, recent efforts to build lightweight inductive learning interventions where participants simply classify real or fake news headlines have either observed null effects (Modirrousta-Galian et al., 2023), or small and inconsistent effects (Modirrousta-Galian et al., 2026). Instead, the current study adopts an approach more consistent with the logic of category induction. Rather than labeling content as true or false, students practice categorizing social media posts according to manipulation techniques that are emblematic of misinformation.

The manipulation techniques selected for the current study were ad hominem attacks, emotional language, false dichotomies, scapegoating, and incoherence (defined in Table 1).

These categories have the practical advantage of being the same manipulation techniques used by Roozenbeek et al. (2022), and thus, high-quality efficacious instructional videos were available for the current study's active control condition. These five categories also have advantages of being hallmarks of manipulative rhetoric as commonly found on social media (e.g., Atlani-Duault et al., 2020; Bradley et al., 2017; Lewandowsky, Cook, & Lloyd, 2016; Simchon, Brady, & Van Bavel, 2022), and they have stable and discriminable semantic features (Molina et al., 2021; Moore & Motz, 2026). Furthermore, these are precisely the features that tend to trigger fast, intuitive, and often inaccurate judgments of online misinformation (Borukhson et al., 2022; Van Bavel et al., 2021). By practicing categorizing social media posts according to these five categories, students might induce an implicit ability to quickly recognize some of the most common and effective forms of manipulative content that tend to appear on social media.

We compare this category induction approach against an active control condition where students learned the same manipulation techniques using a more conventional educational approach (video and multiple choice questions), in an experiment spanning multiple weeks of homework assignments in two cohorts of 9th-grade Civics classes. While these treatments differ in many aspects beyond inductive practice (the use of video in the control condition, the number of practice items, the number of response options on each item, etc.), both conditions were earnestly designed to provide high-quality information literacy instruction. In doing so, the current study makes a pragmatic comparison between competing instructional strategies, which, when real students are involved, can be more appropriate than a comparison against a depleted control condition (Motz et al., 2018). The pragmatic comparison also provides a substantially more rigorous test of the benefits of the category induction approach. Rather than comparing against no treatment or a placebo, the current study examines whether category induction improves misinformation resistance above and beyond a reasonable alternative pedagogical approach to the same material.

The current study's analyses are organized around the following research questions. The first two research questions (RQ1 and RQ2) examine the treatment characteristics of the homework assignments, while the remaining four research questions (RQ3 through RQ6) examine target measures assessed on pretest and posttest surveys: RQ1. How did students' categorization performance change over five weeks of category induction practice? RQ2. How did participants engage with the active control assignments, and how did their engagement compare with the category induction assignments? RQ3. Does category induction affect conceptual knowledge outcomes, compared with the active control condition? RQ4. Does category induction improve veracity discernment on a transfer task (the MIST), compared with the active control condition? RQ5. Does category induction change willingness to share manipulative vs. credible social media posts, compared with the active control condition? RQ6. Does category induction shift metacognitive self-assessments, compared with the active control condition?

If resistance depends mainly on declarative knowledge and deliberately applied procedures (as predicted by theories focused on building an evaluative mindset), the active control should match or beat induction. This holds especially for conceptual knowledge outcomes (RQ3), where the posttest items are taken straight from the control assignments.

However, if misinformation resistance depends on sensitivity to features acquired from examples (as predicted by the induction account), induction should improve discernment (RQ4) and reduce sharing of trained manipulative posts (RQ5), with no advantage in declarative knowledge.

Research Infrastructure

We used Terracotta (Motz et al., 2024) to conduct the current study. Terracotta is an open-source plugin to a learning management system (LMS) that facilitates experimental research on class assignments. Research previously conducted within Terracotta (e.g., Motz et al., 2024; Motz et al., 2025) was limited to a set of standard assignment elements that could be crafted within Terracotta (e.g., multiple choice questions, video playback, short answer questions, file upload). However, Terracotta now supports external integrations; any interactive web object can be seamlessly rendered in an experimentally manipulated LMS assignment, with the standard suite of LMS settings (grade weight, open dates, due dates). Thus, we were able to design a completely novel experimental treatment, a category induction activity with randomly sampled stimuli, and then seamlessly deploy this activity in students' routine coursework in the LMS. Terracotta provides a secure gateway between the LMS and our experimental learning activity, keeping track of which students should be exposed to which version of each LMS assignment (see Figure 1). Importantly, this gateway preserves student privacy. Terracotta never exposes student identities to external web objects; instead, Terracotta uses anonymous identifiers and single-use tokens to manage individual students' assignment launch, submission, and grading, enabling deidentified analyses on the logs of students' actions and behaviors in the external activities. With these features, Terracotta provides instrumentation for an education research test bed, a way to conduct research on novel student learning activities in authentic education settings safely and securely.

Method

The current study was exploratory. All procedures were performed in compliance with an Exempt protocol approved by the Indiana University Institutional Review Board (IRB; protocol #25672, approved on December 3, 2024), and the South Fayette Township School Board. All materials and data are publicly available at https://osf.io/cknrg/.

Participants

All students who were enrolled in 9th grade Honors Civics at South Fayette High School during Spring 2025 (124 enrolled) and Spring 2026 (104 students enrolled) were invited to participate (228 cumulative enrollment), all taught by the same instructor. Of these, 191 students volunteered for the current study and were not opted-out by their parents (91 in Spring 2025; 100 in Spring 2026), for a participation rate of 83.8%. There were no exclusions; all available data from participating students are included in all analyses.

Participants self-reported an average age of 14.5 years (SD = 0.5 years); gender identities that were 48.4% female, 50.0% male, and 1.6% other; and racial identities that were 55.7% White, 32.8% Asian, 1.6% Black, and 9.8% other. While these teenagers' political identities are still developing, 35.0% of participants self-reported being Liberal, 34.4% self-reported being Moderate, and 30.6% self-reported being Conservative.

Materials

For the category induction task, we fabricated a large bank of AI-generated social media posts, where each post was designed to be emblematic of precisely one manipulation technique, or none in the case of the control items, allowing for clean separation across categories. Full details about the generation of these posts, and findings from an unpublished validation study with human raters, are available in Moore & Motz (2026). These features included Ad Hominem, Emotional Language, False Dichotomy, Incoherence, and Scapegoating (see Table 1), along with additional categories in the full validated bank.

For the active control task, we used the five videos from Roozenbeek et al. (2022), each about one and a half minutes in length (between 1:30 and 1:48), and we composed eight original multiple choice questions corresponding to each video. The multiple choice questions assessed students' understanding of the concepts in the video, including the general structure of the manipulation technique, the mechanism involved in the manipulation, and the consequences of susceptibility. Importantly, these questions never gave any direct examples of the manipulation technique, so that the active control task would not support category induction.

Measures

In addition to the responses and behavioral records generated by the category induction and active control tasks, we also administered a pretest and posttest. The sections of the pretest and posttest were presented in the order described below, with items randomized within sections where noted.

Pretest Measures

The pretest survey allowed us to establish a baseline measure of participants' selfreported willingness to share different kinds of social media posts (relevant to RQ5). Ten social media posts were presented in a random order, five included the features of misinformation that participants would subsequently learn in weekly activities (one for each feature; "Misinformation Trained"), two contained no features of misinformation ("No Misinformation"), and three contained features of misinformation that would not be included in the weekly assignments.

These three untrained manipulation tactics -treating a sequence of events as proof that the first caused the second (Post Hoc Ergo Propter Hoc), claiming that a modest step will inevitably set off a chain of extreme consequences (Slippery Slope), and misrepresenting an opponent's position so that it is easier to attack (Straw Man) -are common on social media and relevant to misinformation resistance (e.g., Biddlestone et al., 2025; Irving et al., 2022; Vrbová et al., 2021) but were distinct from the five misinformation tactics included in the weekly learning activities.

For each post, participants rated their agreement with the statement, "I would share this post with people in my network," on a 7-point Likert scale. To simplify the analyses, we treat participants' ordinal ratings as numbers, with larger numbers indicating stronger agreement and thus stronger willingness to share.

The pretest also included five metacognitive items (relevant to RQ6), assessing participants' self-reported ability to identify misinformation, perceived helpfulness of class activities toward helping identify misinformation, perceived importance of being aware of misinformation, class performance, and exposure to misinformation online. Participants responded to these on a 5-point Likert scale of agreement.

Finally, the pretest also included demographic questions (age, gender, race, and political identity), and questions about social media use.

Posttest Measures

The posttest started with a set of ten novel social media posts (order randomized), which invited participants to again rate the likelihood they would share with people in their network, in the same format as the pretest.

The posttest then presented ten items from the 20-item MIST (Maertens et al., 2024; relevant to RQ4) in a random order. Each item is a news headline, and participants mark whether the headline is "Real" or "Fake." The MIST-20 is constructed with matched odd and even halves, with each half containing five real and five fake headlines, with comparable psychometric properties between the halves. To keep posttest length modest while distributing any item-level idiosyncrasies across the sample, we administered only one half of the MIST-20 to each participant: those with odd-numbered anonymous identifiers in Terracotta received the odd items, and those with even-numbered identifiers received the even items.

The posttest then presented five multiple choice questions from the active control activities, one from each activity (relevant to RQ3), in a random order. These questions were directly sampled from the active control assignments without modification, so for participants in the active control condition, these were repetitions of questions they had seen previously over the course of the preceding five weeks. For participants in the category induction condition, these questions were completely novel. Again, to avoid item-level idiosyncrasies, we selected the two questions from each eight-item set which most directly assessed the definition of each technique; and similar to the MIST, participants received the assignment question that matched the parity of their anonymous identifier (odd or even).

The posttest then presented the same five metacognitive self-assessment items as in the pretest.

Procedure

This study was conducted across two separate cohorts (Spring 2025 and Spring 2026), with an identical procedure for each cohort. At the start of the semester, the teacher described the study, both in class and in an email announcement to students' parents. Students registered their decision to participate (or not) by responding to an assent assignment in the LMS, and parents could opt their children out by contacting the research team directly, which would override the student's assent. Students then completed weekly extra credit assignments, generally due Fridays at the start of the school day, with minor modifications for weather closures and holidays.

The first assignment was the pretest survey, rendered as a graded LMS assignment using Terracotta, and students received full credit merely for completion.

For the following five weeks, students were randomly assigned to the category induction or active control condition using Terracotta. Screenshots of these assignments are shown in Figure 2.

In the category induction condition, students saw a social media post and were instructed to categorize it as either Ad Hominem, Emotional Language, False Dichotomy, Incoherence, Scapegoating, or No Misinformation (as defined in Table 1). Students could receive credit for the assignment after achieving 10 correct responses in a row (a "streak" of 10), or after having responded to 30 items in total. On any individual item, if a student responded correctly, the streak counter increased. If a student responded incorrectly, the streak counter reset to 0, and students had to mark the correct category to proceed. We designed this activity with a consecutive correct criterion (a "streak" goal) to disincentivize students from guessing randomly, and so that reaching criterion might take about as much time as active control activities which included short videos (described below); but nevertheless we allowed students to complete the activity after 30 responses to prevent students from becoming stuck trying to achieve criterion. This was not a pure category induction task, because definitions of each category were visible at the bottom of the page. Social media posts were sampled randomly under two constraints: examples from all five categories had to be shown before a category could be drawn again, and posts used in the pretest or posttest were withheld from these weekly assignments.

In the active control condition, students saw a video in an embedded YouTube media player, followed by a set of eight multiple choice questions. Students were instructed to watch the video, then answer the questions. The order of topics for these five weekly assignments were in a fixed order (which had been established at random): Scapegoating, Ad Hominem, Incoherence, Emotional Language, and False Dichotomy. Students answered each multiple choice question, received feedback (with the correct answer revealed for incorrect responses), and had to mark every question correctly to receive credit. The multiple choice questions were designed to assess details, definitions, goals, and consequences described in the videos; these questions never presented any examples of misinformation.

The seventh assignment (after the initial survey and the first five weekly assignments) was the posttest survey, again rendered as a graded LMS assignment using Terracotta, and students received full credit merely for completing the survey.

There was a crossover between the seventh and eighth week of the study, at which time students switched condition assignments. The purpose of the crossover was entirely ethical: to ensure that all participants received all treatments, thus preventing any possibility of biased instruction due to random assignment. Due to the possibility of carryover effects after five weeks of treatment exposure, we focus our attention primarily on data collected during the first half of the semester before the crossover, largely making between-subjects contrasts between students who were initially assigned to category induction assignments and students who were initially assigned to active control assignments.

Data Analysis

The current study was exploratory, and focuses on data collected during the first half of the semester unless otherwise noted. Analyses were scripted in R (R Core Team, 2026), and statistical inference is performed using linear models. When data included multiple observations from each participant over time (RQ1 and RQ5), models were fit using the lmerTest package (Kuznetsova et al., 2017) which extends the lme4 package (Bates et al., 2015). The full analysis script is publicly available at https://osf.io/cknrg/.

The two mixed-effects models (used for RQ1 and RQ5) included random intercepts for each participant. Following Barr et al. (2013), we initially attempted to fit the maximal feasible structure with random slopes for each within-subject predictor, however, in one case (RQ1) the data (which contained only two observations per participant: baseline and week 5) were insufficient to estimate random slopes for each participant, and in the other case (RQ5) a random slope for assessment (pretest vs. posttest) was estimable, but a likelihood ratio test found no improvement with the addition of this random slope, and inferences were unchanged under all alternative random-effects specifications. Thus, for RQ5, we report results for the more parsimonious random intercept model, following Matuschek et al. (2017).

For RQ1, because the category induction task was implemented with a streak criterion, in addition to measuring categorization accuracy, participants' categorization performance can also be examined using survival analysis. Here, survival analysis measures the number of items taken to reach the streak criterion, and we estimate a Cox proportional hazards model using the survival package (Therneau, 2026). "Survival" is operationalized as continuing failure to reach criterion, and thus a lower probability of survival indicates improved performance.

For RQ4, we adopted a signal-detection framework, separately measuring hits (correctly identifying fake headlines as fake) and false alarms (incorrectly identifying real headlines as fake). Veracity discernment (hits minus false alarms) served as the primary outcome, because raw accuracy on the MIST conflates sensitivity to manipulative content with overall response bias (Graham et al., 2023; Modirrousta-Galian & Higham, 2023).

Results

RQ1. Changes in Categorization Performance

The first category induction activity of the semester provides a baseline measure of participants' categorization performance. During this assignment, participants in the category induction condition had average categorization accuracy of 60.5% (SD = 22.5%), and 49.5% of participants achieved the streak criterion of 10 correct responses in a row prior to reaching the maximum 30 trial limit. For participants who achieved 10 correct responses in a row on this first activity, they achieved the criterion after 18.2 trials on average (SD = 6.8).

In their fifth week of categorization practice (week 6; the final activity before the week 7 posttest), participants in the category induction condition had improved to an average accuracy of 77.3% (SD = 27.9%), and 73.9% of participants achieved the streak criterion after 14.2 trials on average (SD = 5.8). In this fifth week of categorization practice, participants had significantly higher odds of responding correctly than at baseline, b = 0.72, z = 8.95, p < .001, OR = 2.06, Participants assigned to the active control condition during the first half of the semester switched into the category induction condition in week 8. Even though we have chosen to focus our analyses primarily on the first half of the semester, these participants present an enticing comparison measure: an estimate of categorization performance following five weeks of direct instruction with multiple choice practice, among participants who had no inductive training. For these participants, average accuracy on their first category induction activity in week 8 was 56.5% (SD = 28.1%), and 42.4% of participants achieved the streak criterion after 17.3 items on average (SD = 7.0). The performance of these participants was directionally worse than the baseline measure of participants who were assigned to category induction condition during the first half of the semester, but neither their accuracy (p = .31) nor speed to criterion (p = .38) is significantly different from the category induction group's baseline measure. Despite weekly learning activities, the active control treatments had no clear benefit for categorization performance.

Participants who were initially in the active control condition and then switched into the category induction activity in week 8 also had significantly worse categorization performance in week 8 than their counterparts in week 6, who had been practicing category induction for the first half of the semester, b = -0.92, z = -4.56, p < .001, OR = 0.40, 95% CI [0.25, 0.64]. They were also slower to reach the criterion, b = -0.94, z = -4.63, p < .001, HR = 0.39, 95% CI [0.26, 0.58]. This is not particularly surprising; it makes sense that learners who did not practice a categorization task would perform worse on the task than learners who did practice it.

Nevertheless, this provides evidence that the category induction condition's improved performance on the categorization task was not incidental, nor can it be attributed to other concomitant activities in the course.

Categorization performance is visualized in Figure 3

RQ2. Performance and Engagement in Active Control Activities

Participants who were assigned to the active control condition watched brief online videos, and then answered multiple-choice questions about them. Each weekly assignment was about a different feature of misinformation, so unlike the category induction condition (where each week was the same activity with different randomly sampled items), we cannot cleanly compare learning performance from week-to-week in the active control.

In the active control condition, 71.9% of participants initiated playback of the online video at least once. In order to receive assignment credit, participants in the active control condition had to mark all questions correctly; and thus by one account, final accuracy was necessarily at ceiling, 100% on all questions. If they initially answered an item incorrectly, a feedback message indicated the text of the correct answer, and then participants had to click the correct answer to proceed. Participants in the active control were correct on their first answer to a question 62.3% of the time (SD = 23.2%), well above chance accuracy (25%), and roughly comparable to baseline accuracy in the category induction condition.

Students in the active control condition completed each weekly assignment in 3.6 min on average (SD = 2.1 min), compared with 4.5 min on average in the category induction condition (SD = 3.5 min)foot_0 . To test this difference in time on task, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with duration (in seconds) as the outcome, condition as the fixed effect, and a random intercept for each participant. Students in the category induction condition spent more time per assignment than students in the active control, b = 53.7 s, 95% CI [9.9, 98.3], t(135.1) = 2.42, p = .017. Thus, time on task is one among several dimensions on which the two pragmatic conditions differed, alongside category sequencing, the presence of distractor (non-misinformation) items, and obviously, the presence of varied examples of misinformation in the category induction condition.

RQ3. Conceptual Knowledge

On the posttest in week 7, all participants responded to five multiple choice questions with no feedback, one about each feature of misinformation, which aimed to assess participants' understanding of the definitions of the features.

Averaging across all participants, overall accuracy on these posttest multiple choice questions was 50.6% (SD = 27.7%), which was considerably lower than students' average performance on first response when these same questions were on the active control condition's weekly assignments (62.3%, noted above). This difference is likely due to the fact that on the posttest, participants earned credit no matter the accuracy of their response (the posttest instructions directly informed them that they would receive full credit no matter their response, but that they should please try to respond correctly), but on the active control assignments, participants had to mark the correct response to earn credit. Even so, average performance on the posttest is still well above chance accuracy (25%), indicating that many participants invested effort in their posttest performance.

At posttest, accuracy for participants who had been in the active control condition was 48.1% (SD = 29.3%), and accuracy for participants in the category induction condition was 52.9% (SD = 26.1%). The difference between conditions was not significant, p = .166. We might interpret this null finding to indicate that participants in the category induction condition were at no measurable disadvantage on assessments of conceptual knowledge (and may have shown a slight directional advantage), compared with students who had previously been exposed to multiple choice practice.

RQ4. Veracity Discernment on the Misinformation Susceptibility Test (MIST)

On the posttest, all participants saw 10 items from the MIST in a random order. Each MIST item is a brief news headline, and participants are instructed to classify the headline as "real" or "fake."

Averaging across all participants, the overall score on the MIST was 6.7 out of 10 items (SD = 2.0). The average number of "hits" (correctly identifying fake headlines as fake) was 3.5 out of 5 (70%; SD = 1.2), and the average number of "false alarms" (incorrectly identifying real headlines as fake) was 1.8 out of 5 (36%; SD = 1.2). Veracity discrimination was operationalized as a discrimination index (hits -false alarms), reflecting the degree to which participants correctly identified fake news (hits) over and above their tendency to reject real news items (false alarms). Participants' average discrimination score was 1.7 (SD = 2.0), on a scale ranging from -5 (perfect inability to discriminate) to +5 (perfect discrimination).

Participants who had been in the active control condition had an average veracity discrimination score of 1.29 (SD = 2.12), and participants who had been in the category induction condition had an average veracity discrimination score of 2.13 (SD = 1.89), shown in Figure 4A (left panel). The difference between conditions is statistically significant, b = 0.84, 95% CI [0.22, 1.46], t(162) = 2.68, p = .008. Overall, category induction improved participants' ability to discriminate between "real" and "fake" news headlines (Cohen's d = 0.42). It is visually apparent in Figure 4A that students in the category induction condition tended to have higher veracity discrimination scores.

Hits (correct identifications of fake headlines) were numerically higher in the category induction condition (M = 3.65/5, SD = 1.13) than the active control (M = 3.35/5, SD = 1.26), and false alarms (real headlines misidentified as fake) were lower (M = 1.52, SD = 1.11 vs. M = 2.06, SD = 1.25). To test whether participants' overall tendency to judge items as fake differed between conditions, we modeled response bias (all "fake" responses: hits + false alarms). The difference between conditions was not significant, b = -0.25, 95% CI [-0.65, 0.14], t(162) = -1.27, p = .21. Thus, category induction improved participants' ability to discriminate real from fake content without making them more indiscriminately skeptical. The shift of the receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve in Figure 4B

RQ5. Willingness to Share

Ideally, school-based information literacy instruction would not only improve learners' ability to discriminate credible claims from those that include manipulation tactics, it would also change their behavior when they encounter it online. We did not measure participants' actual behavior on social media, however, we did measure participants' self-reported likelihood to share social media posts, both on the pretest and the posttest. Overall, participants' mean willingness-to-share rating was slightly below the midpoint, 3.67 (SD = 1.37) on a 7-point scale.

Means were similar across conditions (active control: 3.65, SD = 1.26; category induction: 3.68, SD = 1.47) and across assessment periods (pretest: 3.62, SD = 1.33; posttest: 3.72, SD = 1.42).

As might be expected, willingness to share varied considerably by post category: participants were least willing to share misinformation trained posts (mean = 2.99, SD = 0.94) and misinformation untrained posts (mean = 3.26, SD = 1.08), and most willing to share posts containing no misinformation (mean = 4.76, SD = 1.33).

We fit a 3-way fully-crossed mixed effects linear model, with three fixed effects: condition (active control vs. category induction), assessment (pretest vs. posttest), and category (misinformation trained, misinformation untrained, no misinformation); and a random intercept for each participant. The model revealed a significant three-way interaction between assessment, condition, and category, F(2, 850.98) = 5.40, p = .005, partial η² = .01. We decomposed this interaction by examining pairwise condition differences (active control vs. category induction) separately at pretest and posttest for each post category.

For misinformation trained posts, the two conditions did not differ at pretest (b = 0.14, 95% CI [-0.19, 0.48], t(791) = 0.86, p = .393), and neither showed a significant within-group change from pretest to posttest (p's > .125), but at posttest the category induction group was significantly less willing to share than the active control group (b = -0.49, 95% CI [-0.87, -0.16], t(843) = -2.85, p = .005, d = 0.51). For misinformation untrained posts, there were no condition differences at either pretest (p = .244) nor posttest (p = 0.864). For no misinformation posts, conditions did not differ at pretest (p = .269), but by posttest the category induction group was substantially more willing to share non-manipulative content than the active control group (b = 0.78, 95% CI [0.46, 1.17], t(843) = 4.52, p < .001, d = 0.82). This posttest difference was driven by a significant within-group increase in the category induction condition from pretest to posttest (b = 0.62, 95% CI [0.36, 0.95], t(864) = 4.35, p < .001, d = 0.65), with the active control group remaining flat (p = .873).

Figure 5 visualizes how participants' self-reported willingness to share ratings changed from pretest to posttest, for the two different conditions. Small semitransparent dots stacked horizontally (and their corresponding shaded violins) show the frequency distribution of participants' average willingness to share ratings; heavy markers and error bars show group means and their 95% confidence intervals. It should be visually apparent that participants in the category induction condition self-reported significantly less willingness to share manipulative content that they had been trained to classify, and significantly more willingness to share nonmanipulative content than participants in the active control condition. Category induction not only improved participants' ability to discriminate misinformation (as measured by the MIST, above), it also affected their self-reported likelihood to engage with this content. However, we observed no effect of category induction on willingness to share posts featuring manipulation tactics that were not part of the training curriculum.

RQ6. Metacognitive Self-Assessment

We also examined whether category induction affected participants' self-reported attitudes and awareness, measured by five questions on the pretest and posttest. A MANOVA on pre-to-post change scores for these five items revealed no significant effect of condition, F(5, 155) = 1.19, p = .318, Pillai's V = 0.037, suggesting that the behavioral effects of category induction on veracity discrimination and willingness to share were not accompanied by differences in self-reported metacognitive judgments of misinformation susceptibility.

Discussion

In the current study we tested an inductive learning strategy, where 9th grade students categorized social media posts according to common manipulation techniques, and measured its effect on students' resistance to misinformation, comparing against an active control condition.

Students in the category induction condition demonstrated clear improvements in categorization performance across five weeks of practice. On the posttest, they showed significantly better veracity discrimination on the MIST, were less willing to share manipulative posts they had been trained to identify, and were more willing to share non-manipulative content, relative to students in the active control condition. These behavioral effects were not accompanied by differences in metacognitive self-assessment, nor by any disadvantages on multiple choice assessments of conceptual knowledge. This pattern of results is consistent with a theoretical account in which category induction improves intuitive, feature-driven detection of misinformation. Students in the induction condition became less susceptible to misinformation than students in the control condition, with no measurable differences in declarative knowledge or metacognition. Rather than prompting more deliberation or causing students to adopt an evaluative mindset that would override their intuitions, it may be that inductive training made students' intuitions themselves more accurate. This aligns with recent evidence that individuals' acceptance of misinformation is better predicted by cue-based heuristics than by reasoning alone (Borukhson et al., 2022), and current results further suggest that such heuristic competence can be trained in education settings.

These findings are particularly noteworthy given the nature of the posttest assessment.

The MIST constitutes a relatively distant transfer task for participants in the current study because (a.) questions on the training assignments never included news headlines; (b.) the features of misinformation on the training assignments are not uniformly present in the 'fake' MIST items; (c.) the training assignments never asked participants to evaluate the veracity of a claim (all social media posts in the weekly assignments were known to be fake); and (d.) there was no incentive for accuracy on the posttest. Moreover, the current study provided an exceptionally strong test of this improvement. Even in Civics classes, where all students learned information literacy by default, and when compared with an active control condition, we still see a range of credible improvements when students performed category induction.

Practical Implications

The fact that this effect emerged in an authentic classroom setting is itself a substantive finding. While prebunking interventions have been examined broadly in large samples in the general population, we should not assume an intervention's benefits would be portable across settings (Gopalan et al., 2026). Prior research with online volunteer research participants and very small dosages of inductive training with misinformation headlines found mixed results (Modirrousta-Galian et al., 2023 , 2026). If we aim to improve information literacy instruction, and if psychological science points in a promising direction, we ought to test it in schools (Amar et al., 2026). Indeed, the American Psychological Association recently recommended for researchers to focus efforts at reducing susceptibility to misinformation at early ages (van der Linden, 2025). This study demonstrates a feasible approach, using shared research infrastructure, toward embedding experimental research on novel learning strategies in school settings.

This study also demonstrates the effectiveness of a specific set of student learning activities, which are openly available, can easily be embedded in existing coursework, and can complement lateral reading and information literacy instruction. These may be especially valuable for adolescents, who often encounter news incidentally, and who would benefit from increased sensitivity to the manipulative features that are common in online misinformation.

The current study suggests that inductive learning strategies can improve misinformation resistance, and it stands among a broader chorus advocating for the general utility of category induction tasks in education (Brande & Nosofsky, 2026; Motz et al., 2023; Patterson et al., 2024). But rather than attempting to experimentally isolate the specific effect of induction, we made a pragmatic comparison between two complete instructional strategies, inclusive of additional variability that stems from these strategies (Motz et al., 2018). Specifically, the category induction condition also likely carried the additional benefit of category interleaving (Birnbaum et al., 2013), which is easily afforded by a categorization task where learners classify examples from different categories in a mixed sequence. This likely boosted its benefits compared with the active control, which blocked instruction on the manipulation tactics, one at a time, as each tactic was presented with an instructional video in a single assignment. Furthermore, given that the inductive learning activities included verbal definitions of the categories, we may be measuring a hybrid of inductive categorization practice combined with some declarative knowledge instruction. Students also spent modestly more time with the category induction assignments than the active control assignments. While these factors all comingle in the current study, cumulative evidence suggests that a learning activity organized around category induction can lead to improved performance above a reasonable alternative involving instructional videos and multiple choice questions (see also Redaelli et al., 2025).

Limitations and Future Directions

One possible limitation of the study is that student performance on the posttest assessment had no incentive for accuracy, and indeed, we saw evidence that student accuracy was lower on the posttest than in the weekly assignments (where accurate responses were required for completion). If we had been interested in a more stringent measure of student achievement, we might have embedded the posttest items on a graded exam. However, the ability to identify misinformation should apply outside of graded coursework, and our ungraded posttest on an extra credit assignment, casual as it may have been, may have provided a more realistic measure of transfer to intuitive, real-world information evaluation tasks. Future research might further examine the durability of inductive training on students' actual online behavior, and whether it might affect broader measures of conceptual change (Lombardi et al., 2016).

Another limitation, directly evident in our findings, is that the benefits of category induction did not extend to manipulation tactics outside the training curriculum. Posts featuring untrained manipulation tactics showed no differences in willingness to share between conditions. This specificity is consistent with the view that inductive generalization extends to novel exemplars of trained categories, but does not automatically extend to categories for which no representation exists (Sutherland & Cimpian, 2017). This limitation also helps explain the mixed results observed by Modirrousta-Galian et al. (2023 , 2026), where participants classified headlines using only a binary "real" vs. "fake" classification. A binary distinction provides learners no basis for inducing the structural features that differentiate manipulation tactics from one another, and resulting category representations of general truth and falsehood may be too vague to yield task improvements. Taken together, these findings suggest that inductive training regimens should strive to include as comprehensive a set of manipulation categories as possible.

One alternative worth exploring is to invite learners to construct their own categorizations, a strategy that also yields flexible transfer (Patterson et al., 2024). Whether category construction offers advantages over supervised categorization remains a ripe, open question.

The efficacy of inductive learning may also depend on what learners bring to the task.

Prior knowledge shapes which features learners extract from training examples (Murphy & Medin, 1985; Wisniewski & Medin, 1994), and while these features may be broadly similar across populations (Arechar et al., 2023), the schemas informing our sensitivity to misinformation may be specific to learners' cultural and community experiences (Kumar et al., 2022). The same intervention can still produce different effects across populations (Facciani et al., 2026; Lee et al., 2025). Whether inductive prebunking confers comparable benefits across more diverse student populations, and whether the optimal set of training categories varies across communities, also remains an open question.

Even so, category induction is ill-suited to improve resistance to outright lies, such as false statements that are presented in a purely objective manner, or presented with fabricated evidence. We do not claim that an inductive approach to prebunking is sufficient for resisting all possible forms of misinformation, and becoming sensitive to direct deception may depend on alternative learning strategies (Wineburg et al., 2022). Rather, the inductive approach effectively improves students' ability to discriminate common manipulation techniques, as compared with direct instruction on the manipulation techniques, which we contend to have practical value.

Conclusion

We opened this paper with the claim that democracy depends on information literacy. This general framing is accurate, but it is worth approaching with some nuance, as someone who is skilled at information literacy is not necessarily a well-informed voter. Some scholars have argued that voters can arrive at reasonable decisions through cognitive shortcuts and heuristics; voting as if they were fully informed, even when they are not (Lau & Redlawsk, 1997).

According to this view, what matters is not factual knowledge, but the ability to use available cues to make better judgments, and the current pattern of results is consistent with this more nuanced perspective. Category induction did not significantly improve students' verbalizable knowledge of misinformation, but it did improve sensitivity to the structural features of manipulative content, which is precisely the kind of transferrable, heuristic competence that might underlie the "reasoning voter." If the goal of information literacy education is not to produce misinformation encyclopedias, but to cultivate students who can recognize manipulation when they see it and prioritize information that lacks manipulation, then the current work provides strong evidence that category induction may be a particularly effective strategy. Right panel (B) shows the receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curves for each condition on the MIST. Points mark six performance threshold steps (k = 0 to 5, upper-right to lower-left), where each point represents the proportion of participants exceeding k correct fake news rejections (hit rate) and k incorrect real news rejections (false alarm rate). Error bars indicate 95% bootstrap confidence intervals for each threshold point, and ribbons indicate the hit rate's confidence interval. Self-Reported Likelihood to Share Posts Note. Small semitransparent dots show individual participants' averages within each of the three categories, split by condition, and violins show kernel density estimates of these participants' averages. Heavy markers show the mean for each condition, with error bars indicating 95% confidence intervals. Larger values indicate increased willingness to share a post with their network.
